18131 - STONY POINT NATURE RESERVE: PROPOSED NEW BOARDWALK:
METHODOLOGY STATEMENT – REV. 1
1. INTRODUCTION
This report includes a methodology statement outlining the details of constructing a new boardwalk in the
Stony Point Nature Reserve in Betty’s Bay, South Africa, to replace the existing boardwalk. The detail
designs of the proposed boardwalk are indicated on the relevant engineering drawings and documents. The
methods of construction, the sequence of works and the materials used will be described in detail in this
report. The demolition of the existing boardwalk will also be discussed.
It is important to note that the Stony Point Nature Reserve is known for being home to a large African
penguin colony and special consideration was given to the construction methods in this regard. A segmental
construction philosophy was adopted for this project. This design approach entails the construction of
boardwalk segments off-site with only assembly taking place on site within the shortest time possible. In this
manner, the potential impact on penguins and other wildlife by noise and the presence of construction
workers in the reserve is minimized. In addition, the utilization of similar segments for the design of the
boardwalk greatly improves the simplicity and speed of construction.
1. DESCRIPTION OF WORKS
2.1. Construction sequence
What follows is a detailed description of the works that must be performed during construction, categorised
by element type and discussed in the sequence in which the works will be performed. Refer to the
engineering drawings for additional details on the design of the boardwalk and materials used. A figure is
included at the end of each subsection to graphically indicate the construction step.
It is important to note that approximate lengths and distances included in this report are according to
the design of ‘3m Straight’ segments and are given for descriptive purposes only. All lengths and
distances must be verified with the detail design drawings and will differ for different types of
segments.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

2
2.1.1. Anchoring
The exact coordinates of each proposed boardwalk column (at a spacing of approximately 3m) are shown on
drawing no. 18131-C-011. Columns are mostly located where bedrock is very shallow or at the surface to
minimise any disturbance to the natural ground. A site inspection has confirmed that this will likely always be
the case. If the bedrock is located under a thin layer of sand, the surface must be cleared to expose the
underlying bedrock. At each column location, six holes must be drilled 250 mm deep into the bedrock after
which six Y20 galvanised rebars will be epoxy fixed into the bedrock. The rebars were deliberately designed
to be oversized in terms of structural requirements to allow for a greater surface area for the epoxy material
to bond to the rock and steel. The length of each rebar must be calculated from the vertical alignment design
of the proposed new boardwalk. Rebars must be manufactured to specified lengths and not cut on site to
minimise corrosion potential.
Figure 1. Anchoring of rebars.
2.1.2. Columns
All boardwalk columns will consist of concrete cast in place with the use of shuttering. To create a level
working surface, a rectangular plinth of 700 x 300mm with 25x25mm chamfers must be cast at the base of all
columns up to a point where all bedrock is covered. Figure 2 below illustrates this concept graphically.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

3
Figure 2. Graphical representation of concrete plinths at columns.
On top of each plinth, the remaining section of each column must be cast in place according to a stadium
profile as shown on the engineering drawings. Each column must be cast to a specific height. The height of
each column is indicated on drawings 18131-C-010 and 18131-C-011. At the top of each column, four M20
galvanised bolts must be cast at a depth of 200mm into the column at specific positions. A template plate
can be used to ensure that the bolts cure correctly in place. Care must be taken to ensure minimal wastage
of concrete on the natural ground during the casting of the columns.
After the columns have been cast in place, the only remaining construction is the assembly of
premanufactured elements with bolts.
2.1.3. Main H-beams
After all columns have been cast and adequately cured, the boardwalk must be constructed in segments.
Each of the following steps in the construction sequence must be completed for each individual segment
before repeating the steps for the next segment. To minimize the effect of construction on the natural
environment, all elements of the proposed boardwalk were designed to be light enough to carry onto the site
by hand, removing the need for any heavy machinery.
A 152x152x37 H-beam of approximately 2.33m in length must be affixed to each column via the four M20
bolts that were previously cast into the top of the columns. Each H-beam must be premanufactured to the
exact length and a 450x250x10mm end plate must be pre-welded to the bottom of each beam at the centre
of its length. Additionally, 10mm web stiffeners must be welded to each H-beam at specified locations,
according to the design drawings. Four slotted holes must be pre-drilled in the end plate where it should be
affixed to the concrete column. If the casting of the bolts was done correctly, the H-beam should be carried
into site and simply slide onto the pre-cast bolts. Each bolt must be affixed on both sides of the end plate.
The space between the end plate and the top of the concrete column must be filled with 30MPa non-
shrinkage structural grout.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

4
Each H-Beam will cantilever to both sides of the beam over a distance of 1.165m. During the manufacturing
process, a 154x162x10mm end plate must be welded to each side of each beam, with slotted holes pre-
drilled for the connection of channel beams, discussed in the next section.
Depending on the spacing of the decking planks, discussed later, some slotted holes must be predrilled into
the top flanges of the H-beams at specific locations. Here, the decking planks will be bolted to the beams
instead of wooden joists.
Figure 3. Installation of main H-beams.
2.1.4. Edge channel beams
One 152x76 channel beam will span between the main H-beams on either side of the boardwalk. Like the H-
beams, all channel beams must be premanufactured off-site, hot-dip galvanised and carried into site by
hand. Each channel beam will be approximately 3m in length, depending on the exact spacing and vertical
alignment of the proposed boardwalk design. Each channel beam must be manufactured to the exact
dimensions required for it to fit into its place on the boardwalk. Two slotted holes must be drilled in either end
of a channel beam during the manufacturing process, before galvanisation takes place. Each channel beam
should fit into place between two H-beams and be fixed with two galvanised M16 bolts to the welded end
plates of the H-beams on either side. Four slotted holes should have been predrilled into the endplates.
It is important to note that each channel beam will have specific dimensions and cutting angles, depending
on its position in the boardwalk alignment. Although most channel beams will be straight, some beams will
have to be manufactured at a radius for segments that form a bend. It is imperative that the method used to
manufacture the channel beams at a radius ensures maximum protection against corrosion.
During the manufacturing of the channel beams, slotted holes must be drilled into the top of each channel
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

5
where handrails are to be bolted.
Figure 4. Installation of edge channel beams.
2.1.5. Joists
Timber joists will span between each H-beam parallel to the channel bends, except on bends. Although
channel beams can be manufactured at a radius, timber cannot be bent. At segments where bends occur,
joists must be installed at an angle to the channel beams, depending on the segment design.
Five 150x95mm Rhino Wood beams will span between each H-section. Similar to the channel beams, the
length of each beam will be approximately 3m, but will depend on the exact horizontal and vertical alignment
of each segment. Rhino Wood is an eco-friendly heat-treated and impregnated timber product that is much
stronger than normal timber products. Additionally, it requires minimal maintenance and is very resistant to
moisture ingress and fungal rot and decay.
The joists must be affixed to cleats on the H-beams on either side via side-bolting with galvanised bolts.
Once again, joists must be carried onto site by hand and installed per segment. Holes for the decking planks
to be bolted into place must be predrilled off-site.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

6
Figure 5. Installation of joists.
2.1.6. Decking surface
The deck of the boardwalk will also consist of Rhino Wood. Along the entire boardwalk, segment by
segment, 140x27mm planks must be laid perpendicular to the channel beams on top of the wooden joists.
Each plank will be 2.348m long; the inner width between the channel beams. The length of the planks may
differ on bended segments and embayments. Each plank must be carried onto site and bolted to the main
boardwalk structure through predrilled slotted holes. Where planks run over H-beams they must be bolted to
the beams through predrilled slotted holes.
Figure 6. Installation of decking surface.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

7
2.1.7. Handrails
All handrails will be completely manufactured off site in segments and merely bolted into place on site. Each
handrail segment will be an approximately 1m wide and 1m high segment consisting of 75x20mm steel flat
bars on the top and bottom and 20mm dia. steel solid rods running vertically between the bars at a spacing
of 120mm c/c, welded into place. The rods were deliberately designed to be larger than structurally required
to allow for hot-dip galvanisation and sufficient protection against corrosion. A 20mm thick laser cut steel
plate rail bracket and 38 mm solid steel rod handrail must be welded to the top of each top steel plate. Each
segment of handrail must be galvanised after welding and drilling is completed. Refer to the architect’s detail
design drawings for the handrail details.
Each handrail segment must be manufactured with predrilled slotted holes where it must be bolted to the
channel sections on the main boardwalk structure. It must be noted that handrail segments on bends will be
curved. The exact dimensions of these handrail segments must be determined from the detail design
drawings. Handrails must be bolted to the channel beams of the boardwalks with two galvanised M10 bolts
and a 50x8mm stainless steel fixing plate. A 20mm spacer must be inserted between the bottom of the
handrail flat bar and the top of the channel beam. This will ensure that water is not trapped between the two
steel elements, potentially increasing the risk of corrosion. Where two handrail segments meet, they must be
bolted together at the top with two M10 galvanised bolts and a 50x8mm stainless steel fixing plate.
Figure 7. Installation of handrails.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

8
2.1.8. Other fixtures
After the handrails are installed, the boardwalk structure is practically complete with only minor additional
elements being installed. These include benches and signage and the installation will depend on the design
of the elements. However, these fixtures can be installed after all boardwalk segments are constructed and
will most likely not require any drilling or cutting.
2.2. Embayments
Several embayments are located along the boardwalk alignment to allow for viewing decks. The design
principles are the same for embayments as the main boardwalk alignment. At these locations, an additional
concrete column is designed at an offset of 2.5m from the main alignment column with a 152x152x37 H-
beam spanning between the two columns. On the outer edges of the two columns, the same cantilever
conditions as on the main alignment will apply for the structural elements. The embayments will be
constructed in the same manner as discussed above. The segmental design approach followed allows for
the boardwalk to be extended outwards at sections where embayments are required.
2.3. Construction tolerances
When following a segmental design philosophy, care must be taken that construction work is carried out
accurately to ensure that all elements will align. However, it must always be taken into account that
misalignment may occur to some extent because of the nature of the work. To accommodate for this, the
boardwalk was designed with slotted holes. Slotted holes provide construction tolerances without the need to
cut or weld steel on site, which could lead to higher corrosion potential and noise impact on the sensitive
wildlife in the area.
2.4. Demolition of existing boardwalk
The existing boardwalk will be used as a surface from which construction will take place at most sections of
the new boardwalk, since the alignment of the new boardwalk was designed to follow the existing boardwalk
as closely as possible. As such, the applicable section of the existing boardwalk will be dismantled and
removed by hand after each new section of boardwalk has been constructed. No heavy machinery is
required to demolish the existing boardwalk.
1. ALIGNMENT DESIGN
The alignment design of the proposed boardwalk is greatly influenced by three factors:
3.1. The segmental design approach
The segmental design approach followed in this project places some constraints on the horizontal alignment
of the proposed boardwalk. Because one of the objectives is to simplify the construction process, segment
should not vary significantly in terms of horizontal design. The design goal was to create the horizontal
alignment from straight segments and a maximum of three curved segments with different radii. This had to
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

9
be taken into account when designing the horizontal alignment of the boardwalk to ensure that segments fit.
3.2. Aesthetics
Because the proposed boardwalk is slated to be a tourist attraction, it is imperative that it be aesthetically
pleasing to visitors. This involves the inclusion of smooth curves in the horizontal alignment as opposed to
the jagged edges that the existing boardwalk has on bends. Smoothing out the walking path will also improve
the mobility of visitors on the boardwalk and allow for smooth travel paths for motorized vehicles on the
boardwalk (e.g. motorized wheelchairs and golf carts). Therefore, the horizontal alignment of the proposed
boardwalk was designed to be as smooth as possible while still taking all other design and mobility
considerations into account.
The vertical alignment of the boardwalk was also influenced by the need to provide an aesthetically pleasing
structure. The boardwalk had to be designed in such a manner as to ensure that the boardwalk will be on a
level grade on all horizontal bends, with vertical rises only taking place over straight sections of the
boardwalk.
3.3. Universal access
It is critical to provide universal access to all potential visitors of the boardwalk, including disabled persons.
For this reason, the vertical alignment of the proposed boardwalk was designed to comply to the regulations
set out for the design of structures for disabled persons. This included the requirement that no slope on the
boardwalk may exceed 1:12 and that this maximum slope may only be maintained over a distance of 7.2m
before a flat surface is provided. This restricted both the horizontal and vertical path that could be considered
for the alignment of the proposed boardwalk. At two locations along the boardwalk, the maximum length of
7.2m is exceeded due to rocky outcrops that had to be negotiated. This was, however, discussed with the
client and approved as acceptable.
3.4. Conservation of existing boardwalk path
To minimise the impact on the environment, the alignment of the proposed boardwalk was designed to
conform to the existing boardwalk alignment as closely as possible. It was also important to stay as close to
the existing boardwalk as possible since it will be used as a working surface during the construction phase. It
was, however, not always possible to follow the existing alignment due to the restrictions discussed in the
previous sections. Where this was the case, all possible measures were taken to minimize the distance
between the proposed and existing boardwalks.
1. ENVIRONMENTAL CONSIDERATIONS
4.1. Corrosion protection
The Stony Point Nature reserve is located directly on the coast, leading to a significant risk of corrosion of
steel elements used in the boardwalk. If the steel is sufficiently treated, however, the risk can be adequately
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

10
mitigated. The structural and practical advantages of steel far outweigh other materials that could be used for
the boardwalk and it cannot be justified to use other materials instead of sufficiently protecting the steel with
proper methods.
A double-barrelled corrosion protection approach is implemented to ensure a high protection against steel
corrosion in the harsh climatic conditions. Firstly, all steel elements used in the construction of the boardwalk
must be hot dip galvanised off-site. Additionally, all steel must be painted with a top spec, high quality
marine-grade epoxy paint. If these protection measures are correctly implemented, the maintenance required
to keep the boardwalk in good shape will be minimal.
All steel elements of the boardwalk were slightly oversized in terms of structural requirements to allow for hot
dip galvanisation to be possible and to minimise the risk of structural decay due to corrosion.
Corrosion protection is further ensured by the fact that all drilling and steel cutting will take place in a
controlled environment off-site. This reduces the risk of small section of steel being open to the environment
if it is not adequately protected after cutting or drilling.
4.2. Wildlife conservation and environmental impact
Several environmental constraints need to be considered during the construction of the proposed boardwalk,
even more than other similar projects. This is because the boardwalk is located in the natural habitat of a
large colony of African penguins as well as inside a nature reserve with other sensitive wildlife present. The
main objective of the entire design and construction approach is to minimize the impact on the penguins and
rest of the natural environment.
The segmental design philosophy followed ensures that most of the element construction and manufacturing
takes place off-site. The only construction that will take place in the area of the penguins will be the
anchoring of column rebars in bedrock, the casting of concrete columns and the assembly of the other
elements with bolts. With this approach, noise levels on site is significantly lowered. All elements will also be
carried in by hand, eliminating the need for heavy machinery to be present in the vicinity of the penguins.
The use of the existing boardwalk as a working surface also reduces the number of workers that will be in
the penguins’ habitat.
It is important that all workers on site be mindful of the presence of penguins by working as quietly as
possible and not interacting with the birds. Park rangers and other applicable officials should be on site at all
construction times to guide penguins and ensure that the construction will have the lowest possible impact.
In terms if the impact on the natural environment, the design allows for minimal earth disturbance. The only
disturbance to the natural environment will be the drilling of holes for the anchoring of column rebars. No
other earthworks are required.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)
DIRECTORS: Neil LynersPr Eng FSAICE (cid:127)Mario Filippi Pr Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Fred Laker Pr Tech Eng MSAICE (cid:127)Ermelinde Lyners

11
1. CONCLUSION
This report discusses in detail the construction methodology of a proposed new boardwalk in Stony Point
Nature Reserve in Betty’s Bay, South Africa. The reserve is home to a large colony of African penguins and
a major tourist attraction. Special consideration was given to adopting a design approach that would have
the smallest impact on the penguins and the rest of the natural environment. A segmental approach was
adopted which entails the manufacturing of boardwalk elements in segments off-site. These elements will
then merely be carried into site and bolted in place.
The location of the proposed boardwalk played a major role in the selection and specification of materials
used in the construction of the new boardwalk. The fact that the site is on the shore means that corrosion of
steel and moisture ingress of timber are high risks. Mitigation efforts are, however, included in the
construction methodology.
The main objective of the design philosophy is to reduce the impact of construction on the wildlife in the
reserve and to sufficiently protect the structural elements from the climatic conditions. If all specifications
discussed in this document are followed during construction, it is believed that this goal will be easily
achieved.
Neil Lyners & Associates (RF) (Pty) Ltd (2015/438525/07)

Part 2.11
Decking Installation Guide

RHINO WOOD OR EQUAL AND APPROVED
DECKING
INSTALLATION
The following serves only as a guideline on how to
install Rhino Wood for decking applications
GENERAL INFORMATION/ INTRO
Base construction for decking needs to be done in keeping with and in accordance
to good building practices, standards and regulations. It is important to ensure
good drainage and ventilation beneath the structure to avoid pooling of water
and unnecessary moisture collection. A Rhino Wood deck must be installed
with adequate ventilation below the deck to allow a free flow of hot and
cold air from the decking below the decking surface.
We recommend a maximum joist spacing of 450mm CC apart for 20mm
thick material, and a maximum of 600mm CC joist spacing for 27mm thick
material.
A fully treated CCA sub-structure is recommended. Class 4 treated timber should
always be used when in contact with the ground. Galvanized steel or aluminum
may be used as an alternative to Class 4 timber and the same standards of good building
practices apply.
MOUNTING/ FIXING
Screw fixing
Due to the hardness of Rhino Wood decking timber, it is essential to pre-drill prior to fixing the
decking boards down onto the substructure. Screwing down boards without pre-drilling a pilot hole
MAY result in the boards cracking.
We highly recommend that a pilot hole be drilled, no more than half a millimeter smaller than the
diameter of the fixing screw. If the pilot hole is too small it MAY result in the boards being forced
apart, especially towards the board ends.
* You may also want to consider using the 135mm x 27mm Heavy Duty Rhino Wood deck board
for secret fixing applications as it is suitable for the side grooving necessary for some secret fixing
applications.

RHINO WOOD OF EQUAL AND APPROVED
JOINING OR BUTT JOINING
When butt joining the timber, we recommend using
a double joist on the butt join, in order to have enough
surface area to fix the boards correctly to the sub- structure.
When installing with screws, it is recommended that the
spacing be a minimum of 20mm from the edge of the
boards.
DIFFERENT RHINO WOOD FINISHES
We typically offer three different types of finishes:
Smooth
Rhino Wood finishes to a smooth clean finish that will
surface weather to a grey patina over time.
Brushed
Our most popular finish due to the unique modified nature
of Rhino Wood is brushing. This textured finish adds to the
visual appeal of the boards and is also the option to select
if you want a no-slip surface. .
Reeded
Rhino Wood Boards can be reeded on request.
TESTS & CERTIFICATIONS
Rhino Wood has been tested for a number of performance characteristics both locally and
abroad including dimensional stability, durability, weather resistance and termite damage.
Rhino Wood’s improved characteristics are the result of extensive study and research into
timber modification. The test and certifications provide proof of Rhino Wood’s claim that it
represents a revolution in wood modification. Please view the tests conducted to date by various
institutions by clicking on this link: http://www.rhinowood.co.za/tests_and_certifications/
Rhino Wood will continue to conduct tests on its products into the future and will publish all
test results on this page. If there is a test you would like conducted or you require any further
information please contact scott@rhinowood.co.za

SITE DETAILS, SITE ASSESSMENT AND CLIENT INDENTIFIED HAZZARDS
Project Number:25139C – Stoney Point Boardwalk Replacement
Project Address:Stoney Point Nature Reserve (Penguin Sanctuary), Stoney Point Road, Betty's Bay,
Overstrand Municipality, Western Cape, South Africa
Project Description / Scope of Work
The project entails the demolition and replacement of the existing timber boardwalk within the Stoney
Point Nature Reserve Penguin Sanctuary. The works are located within an environmentally sensitive
coastal conservation area and will be undertaken in a manner that minimizes disturbance to the African
Penguin colony and surrounding natural habitat.
Scope of Work
1. Site Establishment
Establish temporary site facilities.
Implement environmental protection measures.
Install temporary barriers, signage and public exclusion zones.
Maintain controlled pedestrian access where required.
1. Demolition of Existing Boardwalk
Careful dismantling and removal of sections of the existing timber boardwalk.
Segregation and removal of demolition waste.
Transportation and lawful disposal of demolition materials.
Protection of sensitive environmental areas during demolition activities.
1. Rock Drilling and Anchor Installation
Survey and setting out of new boardwalk alignment.
Drill holes into existing rock formations.
Install epoxy-fixed galvanized reinforcing bars into the rock substrate to form foundations for boardwalk
support columns.
Quality control and testing of anchor installations.
Page 2 of 43

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION

STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
1. Construction of Concrete Support Columns
Construct reinforced concrete support columns founded on drilled and anchored rock formations.
Install formwork and cast concrete plinths/columns.
Cure concrete and prepare for installation of prefabricated structures.
1. Installation of Prefabricated Boardwalk Structure
Delivery and handling of prefabricated boardwalk components.
Installation of galvanized steel beams and support members.
Installation of edge channel beams and structural connections.
Bolting and securing structural members to concrete supports.
1. Decking Installation
Installation of Rhino Wood (or approved equivalent) decking planks.
Alignment and securing of decking components.
Installation of connection hardware and finishes.
1. Handrails and Safety Features
Installation of handrails and edge protection systems.
Installation of viewing platforms, embayment sections and landing areas where indicated on drawings.
Installation of signage and other associated fixtures.
1. Environmental Rehabilitation
Removal of construction waste and temporary works.
Restoration of disturbed areas.
Final cleaning and handover.

Hazardous Chemical Agents:Hazardous Chemical Agents such as: solvents, cement, grout, fuels, oils,

Plant on site: Daily inspection registers to be completed. No operator will be allowed on site without his

Working at Height: All activities at height to be done as per fall protection plan and risk assessment. All
documentation to be assessed and approved before working at height commences. Working at height
will be included but not limited to: Working on Scaffolding, Working on Ladders, Erecting and
dismantling of Temporary Works etc.
Environmental Management plan: Some snakes may be present on site. The Principal Contractor is to
ensure that all workers adhere to environmental procedures. No snakes may be killed or harmed. All
workers to receive a toolbox talk on snakes and a signed register is to be placed on file. No fires will be
allowed on site.
ScaffoldScaffold must be erected and inspected by competent employees. No employees may be
allowed to work on unsafe scaffold, the scaffold must be signed off and safe to work signage displayed
before any work commences. Safety Harnesses must be worn at all times by all employees working at
heights.
Excavations and Trenches: All excavations to follow construction regulations. Daily registers must be
completed. Appointed contractor must ensure that all excavation work is carried out under the
supervision of a competent person who has been appointed in writing for that purpose; and evaluate, as
far as is reasonably practicable, the stability of the ground before excavation work begins.The expected
depth of excavations on site will be 2m. Shoring, bracing and permit to dig will be required by the
Principal Contractor.
Traffic management: Delivery of materials and the safe movement of construction vehicles will be
controlled by means of a traffic management plan to alleviate any congestion or interference.

1. Introduction and Background
1.1 Background to the Health and Safety Specification (also termed This Specification)
The Construction Regulations (February 2014) place the onus on the Client to (a) prepare a baseline risk
assessment for an intended construction work project; (b) prepare a suitable, sufficiently documented
and coherent site-specific health and safety specification for the intended construction work based on
the baseline risk assessment contemplated in paragraph (a).
1.2 Purpose of the Health and Safety Specification
To assist in achieving compliance with the Occupational Health & Safety Act 85/1993 (OHS Act) and the
now promulgated Construction Regulations (February 2014) in order to prevent or as far as possible,
reduce incidents and injuries. This specification shall act as the basis for the drafting of the Principal
Contractor’s and Contractors construction phase health & safety plans.
The health & safety specification sets out the requirements to be followed by the Principal Contractor
and other Contractors so that the health & safety of all persons (including the public) potentially at risk
may receive the same priority as other facets of the project e.g. cost, programmer, environment, etc.
The entire document is to be considered
1.3 Implementation of the Health and Safety Specification
This health & safety specification forms an integral part of the contract, and the Principal Contractor is
required to use it when drawing up its project-specific construction phase health & safety plan. The
Principal Contractor must forward a copy of this specification to all Contractors at their bidding stage so
that they can in turn prepare a health & safety plan relating to their operations.
1. HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
2.1 Scope of the Health and Safety Specification
This Specification covers the requirements for eliminating and mitigating incidents and injuries on this
Project.
The scope also addresses legal compliance, hazard identification and risk assessment, risk control, and
the promotion of a health and safety culture amongst those working on the project. The health & safety
specification also makes provision for the protection of those persons other than employees.
This Specification aims to address the duties laid upon the Client in terms of the Construction
regulations. The initial risk assessment attached to this document is deemed a base-line risk assessment
addressing existing pre-construction hazards and construction stage hazards. The Principal Contractor

and Contractors are required to develop their own project and task specific risk assessment in terms of
Section 9 of the Construction Regulations and General Safety Regulations 2(1).
Section 2.3 of the specification considers all the administrative requirements as well as administrative
processes, which the Principal Contractor must adhere to, or implement prior to and during the
construction period.
Section 2.4 of the specification describes several anticipated requirements in terms of the physical
activities to be undertaken during the construction period. The Principal Contractor is responsible for all
construction related processes as well as the potential impact such process may have on the surrounds
including members of the public.
Section 2.5 covers the requirements in terms of plant and equipment.
This specification attempts to address all the anticipated concerns and requirements however where an
issue, process or plant usage is not covered the Principal Contractor must ensure all legal requirements
are met regardless.
2.2 Interpretations
2.2.1 Application
This specification is a compliance document drawn up in terms of the Occupational Health & Safety Act
no 85 of 1993 (OHSA), Construction Regulation 2014 and is therefore binding. It must be read in
conjunction with all other relevant legislation as noted previously.
2.2.2 Definitions
The definitions as listed in the Occupational Health & Safety Act 85/1993 and Construction Regulation
(February 2014) shall apply.
"Client"means any person for whom construction work is being performed.
"Agent"means a competent person who acts as a representative for a client.
"Principal contractor"means an employer appointed by the client to perform construction work.
"Construction site"means a work place where construction work is being performed.
"competent person"means a person who has, in respect of the work or task to be performed, the
required knowledge, training and experience and, where applicable, qualifications, specific to that work
or task: Provided that where appropriate qualifications and training are registered in terms of the
provisions of the National Qualification Framework Act, 2000 (Act No.67 of 2000)

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),
STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
"Construction Manager"means a competent person responsible for Managing and supervising
construction activities on a construction site.
"Construction vehicle"means a vehicle used as a means of conveyance for transporting persons or
material, or persons and material, on and off the construction site for the purposes of performing
construction work.
"Construction work"means any work in connection with- The construction, erection, alteration,
renovation, repair, demolition or dismantling of or addition to a building or any similar structure; or the
construction, erection, maintenance, demolition or dismantling of any bridge, dam, canal, road, railway,
runway, sewer or water reticulation system; or the moving of earth, clearing of land, the making of
excavation, piling, or any similar civil engineering structure or type of work.
"Contractor"means an employer who performs construction work.
"Design"in relation to any structure, includes drawings, calculations, design details and specifications.
"Designer"means a competent person who-
(cid:131) prepares a design;
(cid:131) checks and approves a design;
(cid:131) arranges for a person at work under his or her control to prepare a design, including an
(cid:131) Employee of that person where he or she is the employer; or
(cid:131) designs temporary work, including its components;
(cid:131) an architect or engineer contributing to, or having overall responsibility for a design;
(cid:131) a building services engineer designing details for fixed plant;
(cid:131) a surveyor specifying articles or drawing up specifications
"Fall protection plan”means a documented plan, which includes and provides for- (a) all risks relating to
working from a fall risk position, considering the nature of work undertaken; (b) the procedures and
methods to be applied in order to eliminate the risk of falling; and (c) a rescue plan and procedures; “fall
risk” means any potential exposure to falling either from, off or into.
"Health and safety file”means a file, or other record containing the information in writing required by
these Regulations.
"Health and safety plan"means a site, activity or project specific documented plan in accordance with
the client's health and safety specification.
"Health and safety specification"means a site, activity or project specific document prepared by the
client pertaining to all health and safety requirements related to construction work.

"Bulk mixing plant"means machinery, appliances or other similar devices that are assembled in such a
manner so as to be able to mix materials in bulk for the purposes of using the mixed product for
construction work.
"Material hoist"means a hoist used to lower or raise material and equipment, excluding passengers.
"Medical certificate of fitness"means a certificate contemplated in regulation 7(8).
"Mobile plant"means any machinery, appliance or other similar device that is able to move
independently, and is used for the purpose of performing construction work on a construction site.
"scaffolding"means a temporary elevated platform and supporting structure used for providing access
to and supporting workmen or materials or both.
"shoring"means a system used to support the sides of an excavation and which is intended to prevent
the cave-in or the collapse of the sides of an excavation.
"Structure"meansany building, steel or reinforced concrete structure (not being a building), railway line
or siding, bridge, waterworks, reservoir, pipe or pipeline, cable, sewer, sewage works, fixed vessels,
road, drainage works, earthworks, dam, wall, mast, tower, tower crane, bulk mixing plant, pylon, surface
and underground tanks, earth retaining structure or any structure designed to preserve or alter any
natural feature, and any other similar structure; separate ropes from each support.
"Temporary works"means any falsework, formwork, support work, scaffold, shoring or other
temporary structure designed to provide support or means of access during construction work.
"The Act"means the Occupational Health and Safety Act, 1993 (Act No. 85 of 1993)

2.3 Minimum Administrative Requirements
2.3.1 Health and Safety File
The Principal Contractor and all contractors are responsible for the establishment and maintenance of a
Health and Safety File. This file will be a compilation of all health and safety documents related to or
generated during the construction phase. This file must be kept safe and on site at all times. On
completion of the project the consolidated Safety File must be handed to the Safety Representative for
review and presentation to the Client.
2.3.2 Health & Safety Management Plan
The Principal Contractor and all contractors are required to implement a project specific health & safety
management plan. The health & safety plan assessment process considers the content of the plan as
well as the commitments made their-in where the objective of the assessment process is to determine
whether or not the contractor understands health & safety planning / management and acknowledges
their responsibility to ensure the effective management thereof.
The following list presents the contractor with a guideline of what critical aspects we intend measuring
or assessing during the safety plan approval process:
Scope of works – a clear description of how the work will be done.
Site establishment – site yard and site office setup as well as access control and public safety.
Site Management & Supervision – not only the appointment of a manager, supervisor and Safety Officer
but a clearly defined commitment to ensure the appointed person is on site at all times and competent
where competence relates to health & safety management.
Risk Assessments – the contractor needs to provide a risk assessment as well as describe how their
management system allows for the on-going assessment of risks specific to tasks on site.
Training – the plan must define the contractor’s commitment to training: induction training, toolbox talk
training, key competency training and risk specific training.
Contractor selection – a description of the process of selecting contractors which specifically includes
safety plan approvals and proof of good standing with workman’s compensation.
Contractor management – what systems will be implemented to manage and monitor compliance
among contractors on a continual basis including the stoppage of unsafe activities and situations. The
Client/OHS Agent, Principal Contractor and Department of Labour may stop work immediately if there is
a life threating situation or non-compliance with this Specification or Principal Contractor OH&S Plan.
Page 13 of 43

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),
STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
Stop order will be issued by the Principal Contractor. The contractor must provide an action plan to
prevent reoccurrence. Non-compliance will be discussed at the site meeting.
Emergency management and first aid provisions
Inspection records – a commitment to ensure that all plant and equipment is inspected and maintained
including incident management and the reporting, investigation, and improvement processes to be
implemented by contractors.
Fall Protection Planning & Edge Protection – a description of how they intend managing all
penetrations, scaffolds, and ladders: it is important to describe what materials will be used.
Plant and Equipment – the plan needs to describe the contractor’s commitment to the provision of
suitable equipment relative to the work being performed, the competence of the workers making use of
the plant and equipment, the maintenance of such plant and equipment.
PPE – a commitment to provide and ensure the use of PPE needs to be described.
The health & safety management plan including Risk Assessments, Method Statements and Fall
Protection Plan must be electronically emailed to the Client Representative within 7 days before
Commencement of work for approval. All contractors will be required to forward their health & safety
management plan to the Principal Contractor within 7 Days before commencement of work for
approval. The Safety plan and Approval letter must form part of the Safety file.
2.3.3 Notification of Construction Work
The Principal Contractor must notify The Department of Labor in writing, (Annexure 2) seven (7) days
before construction work commences. Proof thereof must be electronically sent to the Clients
Representative a copy of this notification and proof thereof must form part of the Safety File on Site.
2.3.4 Assignment of the Principal Contractor’s / Contractor’s Responsible Persons to
Supervise Health and Safety on Site
The Principal Contractor and all Contractors must make all relevant appointments in writing (as
stipulated the OHSA, Construction Regulations 2014 and all other relevant regulations/Acts/SANS etc.),
prior to commencement of work.

2.3.5 Competence of the Principal Contractor’s / Contractor’s Appointed Competent
Persons
The Principal Contractor’s and all Contractor’s will make use of a competent person for the various risk
management portfolios and must fulfill the criteria as stipulated under the definition of ‘Competent’ in
accordance with the Construction Regulations (February 2014). Proof of competence must be attached
to their appointment letters
The Principal Contractor must note that the Construction Site Manager – CR 8(1); being the most senior
company representative on site is ultimately responsible to implement the company’s health & safety
plan and all related procedural documents and therefore he must be competent in terms of safety
management. The appointed person will also be responsible to ensure that all contractors are effectively
managed in terms of health & safety compliance.
The Client OH&S Agent reserves the right to instruct the Principal Contractor or Contractors to assign an
alternative Construction Site Manager and/or Supervisors to the project.
2.3.6 Compensation for Occupational Injuries and Diseases Act 130 of 1993 (COIDA)
This could also be a Letter of Good Standing with FEM (Federated Employers Mutual). The Principal
Contractor and Contractors shall have in its possession a letter of good standing with its Compensation
assuror as proof of registration. The Principal Contractor and Contractors must before commencement
of work electronically forward the Letter of Good Standing to the Clients Representative. A VALID Copy
thereof must form part of their Safety Files.
The Principal Contractor OR Contractors WILL NOT be ALLOWED TO COMMMENCE WORK WITHOUT
Proof of registration
2.3.7 Occupational Health and Safety Policy
The Principal Contractor and all Contractors shall submit a Health and Safety Policy including but not
limited to: Alcohol and Drug Policy, Environmental Policy, & PPE Policy, these policies must be
communicated to all employees and be signed by its Chief Executive Officer. The Policy should outline
the Employer’s objectives as well as how they will be achieved and implemented by the Employer.
2.3.8 Health and Safety Organogram
The Principal Contractor and all Contractors shall prepare an organogram, outlining the site
management health & safety structure including the relevant appointments/competent persons. In
Page 15 of 43

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),

cases where appointments have not been made, the organogram shall reflect the intended positions.
The organogram must be updated when there are any changes in the Site Management structure.
2.3.9 Preliminary Hazard Identification and Risk Assessment, Progress Hazard
Identification and Risk Assessment, Risk Reviews, Monitoring and DSTI
The Principal Contractor must in writing appoint a competent person Knowledge training and Experience
(Valid Certificate) before commencement of work, the Risk Assessments shall form part of the health
and safety plan applied on site. This risk assessment will need to be comprehensive and may well lead
to further, separate, more in-depth risk assessment studies. Should the Principal Contractor commence
work without approval of the risk assessment, or should the risk assessment not reflect the activities
being undertaken, The Client Representative may instruct the work to be immediately halted, and The
Principal Contractor will have no claim against the Client and the same for the Contractors, in such a
case for lost time or costs, irrespective of whether it can be demonstrated that the work was being
safely undertaken.
The risk assessment should include the following:
(cid:131) The identification of the risks and hazards to the health and safety to which persons may be
exposed;
(cid:131) The analysis and evaluation of the hazards identified;
(cid:131) a documented plan and safe working procedures to mitigate, reduce or control the risks
identified; and
(cid:131) The monitoring and review plan of the risks and hazards.
(cid:131) The relevant personal protective equipment or clothing
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that all Contractors are informed, instructed and trained by
competent persons regarding any hazard and related work procedures before any work commence. The
competent person will be required to review and monitor (Planned Job Observations) the risks on a
monthly basis. The construction Manager 8(1) will be responsible to conduct Daily Safe Task Instructions
with all employees on a daily basis, proof must be kept in the Health and Safety File.
2.3.9.1 Issue Based Risk Assessment
As circumstances and needs arise, separate risk assessment studies will need to be conducted. These will
be associated with a system for the management of change. An additional risk assessment will need to
be conducted and submitted to the Client/Representative for approval when for example:
(cid:131) A new machine is introduced onto site
(cid:131) A system for work is changed or operations altered

25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),
STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
(cid:131) After an accident or a near miss has occurred
(cid:131) New knowledge comes to light and information is received which may influence the level of risk
to employees on site.
2.3.9.2 Continuous Risk Assessment
This is the most important form of risk assessment which should take place continually, as an integral
part of day-to-day management. This should be conducted by the Safety Officer, management and
supervisors on site and it is essential that formal training be provided to enable this process to be
efficient. Examples of Continuous Risk assessment include:
(cid:131) Audits
(cid:131) DSTI
(cid:131) General hazard awareness linked to a suggestion scheme
(cid:131) Pre-work assessments using checklists.
In general, the Contractor must ensure that the Risk Assessment involves identifying the hazards present
in a work activity on site. This is followed by an evaluation of the extent of the risk involved taking into
account those precautions already being taken.
The Contractor shall be responsible for making sure that all employees under his/her control are
conversant with the content of the Risk Assessment and what appropriate measures have been put in
place to either eliminate or reduce the identified risks. The Contractor shall outline to employees what
role they are expected to play in the Risk Assessment and control measure process.
2.3.10 Health and Safety Training
2.3.10.1 Induction
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that all site personnel undergo a site-specific health & safety
induction training before commencement of work. Each contractor must induct their own personnel
specific to their hazards, activities, conditions. Storage and Control of Hazardous Chemical Agents, Site
rules, Welfare Facilities, Emergency procedures and Method Statements. A record of attendance shall be
kept in the Health & Safety file.
2.3.10.2 Awareness
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that, on site, periodic toolbox health & safety talks take place at
least once every week – these training talks form part of the Daily Task Risk Assessment process.
Training talks should deal with risks relevant to the construction work in process, conveying safe work
Page 17 of 43

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION

STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
procedures to the employees involved. A record of attendance shall be retained within the health &
safety file. All Contractors have to comply with this minimum requirement.
2.3.10.3 Competence
All competent persons must have the knowledge, experience, training, and qualifications specific to the
work they have been appointed to supervise, control, and carry out. This will have to be assessed on a
regular basis e.g. training, evaluation, and periodic audits by the Client, progress meetings, etc. The
Principal Contractor is responsible to ensure that competent Contractors are appointed to carry out
construction work.
2.3.11 General Record Keeping
The Principal Contractor/Contractors shall keep and maintain Health and Safety records to demonstrate
compliance with this Specification, with the OHS Act 85/1993, and with the Construction Regulations
(February 2014). The Principal Contractor shall ensure that all records of incidents/accidents, emergency
procedures training, inspections, audits, etc. are kept in a health & safety file held in the site office. On
Completion of the project the Health and Safety File must be handed over to the Client/ Client
Representative.
2.3.12 Health & Safety Audits, Monitoring and Reporting
The Client Representative shall conduct Monthly ad hoc Health & Safety audits on the safety
management system as well as the physical site activities (No notification will be given to ensure
compliance). The Principal Contractor: is obligated to conduct similar audits on all Contractors appointed
by it and keep audit reports in its health & safety file. All audit reports must be circulated / distributed to
the relevant parties / persons.
2.3.13 Emergency Procedures
The Principal Contractor must prepare a detailed Emergency Procedure prior to commencement on site.
The procedure shall detail the response plan including the following key elements:
(cid:131) List of key competent personnel
(cid:131) Details of emergency services
(cid:131) Actions or steps to be taken in the event of the specific types of emergencies;
(cid:131) First Aid Cases
(cid:131) Medical Cases
(cid:131) Fires
(cid:131) Evacuations

(cid:131) Information on any hazardous material/situations.
Emergency procedure(s) shall include, but shall not be limited to: fire; chemical spills; injury to
employees; damage to material/equipment/plant; use of Hazardous Chemical Agents; bomb threats;
major incidents/accidents; etc. The Principal Contractor shall advise the Client in writing forthwith, of
any emergencies, together with a record of action taken. A contact list of all service providers (Fire
Department, Ambulance, Police, Medical and Hospital, etc.) must be maintained and available to site
personnel.
2.3.14 First Aid Boxes and First Aid Equipment
The Principal Contractor and contractors shall appoint a First Aider in writing (If employees exceed more
than 10). Copy of valid certificates is to be kept on site. First Aid Box with relevant signage must be kept
on site (If employees exceed more than 5)
2.3.15 Incident / Injury Reporting and Investigation
The Contractor will ensure that they will appoint an Incident Accident Investigator in writing to
investigate all incidents and near misses. The Contractor will inform the Client Agent as well as the
Department of Labour when required within twenty-four (24) hours. All investigation reports must be
completed and forwarded to the Client Agent within seven (7) working days from the date of the
incident.
Records of incidents as well as investigation reports must form part of the Safety File.
2.3.16 Medical Certificate of Fitness
All contractors must ensure that their employees on site have a valid medical Certificate of fitness,
specific to the construction work being performed, issued by an occupational health practitioner in the
form of an Annexure 3.
2.3.17 Personal Protective Equipment (PPE) and Clothing
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that all site workers are issued with and wear hard hats and safe
footwear. The Principal Contractor and all Contractors shall make provision and keep adequate
quantities of SABS approved PPE on site at all times according to the risk assessments. The Principal
Contractor shall clearly outline procedures to be taken when PPE or Clothing is:
(cid:131) Lost or stolen
(cid:131) Worn out or damaged

The above procedure applies to Contractors and their Contractors, as they are all Employers in their own

necessary PPE to its own employees however; the responsibility to enforce compliance in this regard
remains that of the Principal Contractor. Labor Only Contractors appointed by the Principal Contractor
become the responsibility of the Principal Contractor.
2.3.18 Occupational Health and Safety (OHS) Signage
The Principal Contractor must provide adequate on-site OHS signage. Including but not limited to: ‘no
unauthorized entry’, ‘report to site office’, ‘site office’, ‘beware of overhead work’, ‘hard hat area’.
Signage Emergency Assembly point, Danger, Open Excavation, First Aid Box Signage and Fire
Extinguisher must be posted at all entrances to site as well as on site in strategic locations e.g. access
routes, entrances to structures scaffolding, and other potential risk areas/operations.
Health & safety signage must be well maintained including weekly inspections, cleaning, replacement
and repair.
2.3.19 Permits
The Principal Contractor must ensure that all permit requirements and standards are met and the copies
of all permits obtained or issued are retained on file.
Permits may include the following:
(cid:131) Closure of public walkways, roadways;
(cid:131) Hoarding Permits
(cid:131) Hot-work Permits
(cid:131) Scaffold Handover Certificates
(cid:131) Confined Space Access Permits
2.3.20 Contractors Requirements and Health and Safety File
No contractors are permitted to work on site unless at least one of the Principal Contractors competent
site supervisors is available on site and unless the workers have access to all of the on-site facilities. All
Contractors and contractors must be appointed by the Principal Contractor in writing and copies of
these appointments must be kept on file. The Principal Contractor shall ensure that all Contractors
appointed by it comply with this Specification, the OHS Act 85/1993, Construction Regulations (February
2014), and all other relevant legislation that may relate to the activities directly or indirectly. The
Contractor, when appointing other Contractors as ‘contractors’, shall mutatis mutandis ensure
compliance as if it was the Principal Contractor.

The principal contractor shall take all reasonable steps necessary to ensure co-operation between all

The Principal contractor must ensure that where changes are brought about to the design and

to Contractors so as to allow them to execute the work safely;
The Principal Contractor must ensure that every contractor is registered and in good standing with a
recognized compensation fund or with a licensed compensation insurer prior to work commencing on
site;
The Principal Contractor must ensure that potential Contractors submitting tenders have made provision

and other similar information concerning the completed structure;
The Principal Contractor shall not appoint a Contractor to perform construction work unless the
Principal Contractor is reasonably satisfied that the Contractor, he or she intends to appoint has the
necessary competencies and resources to perform the construction work safely.
2.3.21 Public and Site Visitor Health & Safety
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that every person working on or visiting the site, as well as the
public in general, shall be made aware of the dangers likely to arise from site activities, including the
precautions to be taken to avoid or minimize those dangers. Appropriate health and safety notices and
signs shall be posted up, but shall not be the only health & safety measures taken. Both the Client and
the Principal Contractor have a duty in terms of the OHS Act 85/1993 to do all that is reasonably
practicable to prevent members of the public and site visitors from being affected by the construction
activities. Site visitors must be briefed on the hazards they may be exposed to as well as what measures
are in place or should be taken to control these hazards. As per the Construction Regulations, a record of
these ‘inductions’ must be kept on site (attendance register or visitors’ book with site rules leaflet).
Where scaffolding is erected adjacent to or over public areas, see scaffold requirements 2.4.6 Where

2.3.22 Night Work (Before and After Hours)
The Principal Contractor must ensure that adequate lighting is provided to allow for work to be carried
out safely. Workers must be supervised at all times and contractors are only permitted to work on site
while a competent supervisor representing the Principal Contractor is available on site. Workers must
have access to facilities.
2.3.23 Transport of Workers
The Principal Contractor and other Contractors shall not:
Transport persons together with goods or tools unless there is an appropriate area or section to store
the tools or equipment; Transport persons in a non-enclosed vehicle, e.g. truck; there must be a proper
canopy structure (steel). Workers shall not be permitted to stand or sit at the edge of the transporting
vehicle. (Covering the back and top) with suitable sitting area Transport workers in Pick-up trucks unless
they are closed / covered and have the correct number of seats for the Passengers.
2.3.24 Construction Health & Safety Officer
The Principal Contractor must appoint a Part Time Health & safety Officer. The Health and Safety Officer
must conduct fortnightly (Twice a month) Health and Safety Audits. Each contractor is also required to
appoint their own safety officer. This appointment can be part-time in nature, the site visit intervals
would need to be agreed with the Principal Contractor based on such criteria as: number of site
personnel, work complexity, risk profile, sub-contractor appointed by the contractor, etc. The Health
and Safety Officer must have proof of Registration with the SACPCMP (Up to date letter of Good
Standing)
Audits and inspections including administrative and physical audits of all Contractors’ health & safety
plans, files and activities, and record findings in the form of audit reports to be kept in the health &
safety file; and emailed to the Client Agent.
(cid:131) Maintain the health & safety plan and file;

(cid:131) Co-ordinate the function of reviewing the hazard identifications and risk assessments;

(cid:131) Assisting with method statements, safe work procedures and checking whether the responsible

(cid:131) Ensuring that all OH&S reports and relevant correspondence is issued to the respective party

2.3.25 Penalties
Penalties may be imposed for ongoing non-compliance with the provisions of the Client’s health & safety
specification, the Principal Contractor’s and Contractor’s health & safety plans. The penalty procedure
shall consist of a written warning with a compliance time frame. Non-conformances relating to the
following critical areas may result in the imposition of immediate penalties:
(cid:131) Edge protection standards and other fall risk related standards.
(cid:131) Contractor safety plan approvals
(cid:131) Contractor audits
(cid:131) Induction Training
(cid:131) The on-going development of task specific risk assessments
(cid:131) The effective development of method statements
(cid:131) Plant operator and crane operator appointments, competency and medical fitness
(cid:131) Inspection records relating to plant and small plant.
(cid:131) Management of excavations and inspections relating to excavations
2.3.26 Employee Identification
It is the responsibility of each contractor to ensure that their employees are identifiable. All employees
will be issued with an overall indicating their company name.
A copy of each employees’ ID document must be placed on the Contractors Safety File for record
purposes. These records must be kept up to date.
2.3.27 Traffic Management Plan
It will be required from the Principal Contractor to produce a site-specific traffic management plan
indicating how traffic will be controlled on site. The traffic Management Plan shall ensure that operators
and public do not collide or cause any environmental damage. The traffic management should form part
of the health and safety file and should be displayed on a notice or site container.
2.3.28 Health and Safety Representative and Committee
The Principal Contractor and all Contractors shall ensure that Health and Safety Representative(s) are
appointed under consultation with the employees and trained to carry out their functions. The
Page 23 of 43

25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),
STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
appointments must be in writing. The Health and Safety Representatives shall carry out regular
inspections, keep records and report all findings to the Responsible Person forthwith and at health &
safety meetings. Health & safety representatives are required as soon as an Employer has 20 employees
on site (A Safety Representative will be required once there are 20 employees, up to 50, and then
another as soon as there are 51 employees, and then a 3rd once there are 101 employees). The Principal
Contractor shall ensure that a project health and safety committee meeting is held monthly and minutes
are kept on record. Meetings must be organized and chaired by the Principal Contractor’s Responsible
Person (Section 16(2) person or CR 8(1) person). Site H&S meeting (apart from safety committee
meetings) must be held once a month, chaired by the Principal Contractor and have in attendance all
site supervisors and contractor supervisors. Safety reps and safety officers can also attend however the
site supervisors are the critical attendees.
2.3.29 Pricing for Health and Safety Compliance
The Client shall make provision for the Health and Safety Compliance of the Principal Contractor. The
Principal Contractor shall make provision for all contractors Health and Safety as per Construction
Regulation 2014 (All pricing can be found in the Bill of Quantities)
2.4 Physical Requirements
2.4.1 Site Set-up
The Principal Contractor will be responsible to set-up a suitable site-office and storage area or yard. The
location of such an office must be planned and discussed in advance with the Client.It is of vital
importance that the Principal Contractor considers all environmental issues or concerns during the
planning and implementation process.
The site set-up must consider and include all the requirements in terms of accessibility, facilities, security
and public safety; thereby ensuring that the site is practically laid-out, well organized and accessible at
all times. The perimeter must be secured to effectively control access thereby preventing members of
the public from entering the site without permission. The following non-negotiable provisions must be
established, available and maintained on site;
One toilet per 30 persons on site; a first aid box fully stocked; Fire fighting equipment as per the
requirements as set out in the Principal Contractors fire risk assessment done before the site set-up
commences; all relevant symbolic and descriptive signage; all anticipated Personal Protective Equipment
requirements including at least four (4) full-body harnesses; A notice board for the displaying of safety
related information; a flammable store (where necessary); ladders; temporary walkways constructed as
required; Cement Mixing / Batching area (demarcated); as well as any other facilities which would
contribute to an effective safety management system.

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),

2.4.2 Excavations/Trenching/Foundational Works
The Principal Contractor will appoint in writing a competent Excavation Supervisor writing for this
project. This appointment must form part of the Safety File.
The Principal Contractor must formulate a Safe work Procedure and Method Statement for this Activity.
All excavations must be performed under strict supervision prior to the commencement of construction
activity. All plant operators must be competent and all plant must be in a safe-for-use condition. Plant
must be inspected on a daily basis. These inspections must be recorded on a checklist.
The Principal Contractor and any relevant Contractors shall make provision in their tender for shoring
and bracing of any excavations / trenches as per this specification. Where any uncertainty exists, the
decision of a professional engineer or professional technologist competent in excavation work will be
decisive.
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that:
(cid:131) Excavations/trenches are inspected before every shift and a record of these inspections is kept;
(cid:131) Safe work procedures have been communicated to the workers;
(cid:131) The safe work procedures are enforced and maintained by the Principal Contractor’s and
Contractors’ Responsible Persons at all times;
(cid:131) Safe access is provided;
(cid:131) No load, material, plant or equipment is placed or moved near the edge of any excavation or
trench. A safe distance of no less than 1.5 meters from the edge of the excavation must be
maintained;
(cid:131) The requirements as per section 13 of the Construction Regulations are adhered to.
2.4.3 Existing Structures
The Principal Contractor must hoard the perimeter of the Construction site. Height of the Hoarding to be
decided by design team – At least 1.5m high hoarding is recommended. If applicable the Hoarding must
be designed and signed off by a Structural engineer (Design team to be consulted). Every effort must be
made to protect all existing services, roadways around the site that may be affected by construction
work. Any structures, existing curb-lines, manholes and storm-water drains, entrances etc. must be
checked for any signs of damage prior to commencement and monitored thereafter. Should any damage
be caused to the existing services, roadways or nearby structures, the client in consultation with the
design team will instruct the Principal Contractor on how to proceed. No materials may be stored on any
of the adjacent properties or in any public area.

2.4.4 Edge Protection and Penetrations
The Principal Contractor must ensure that all exposed edges and openings are guarded and demarcated
at all times until permanent railings, barriers or structures have been erected. The temporary rails or
guards must be constructed of scaffold tubing or a material of similar strength and offering similar
protection. Guardrails must be placed at 1m above the platform / floor and must be distinguishable
from the rest of the structure, scaffold or formwork. The Principal Contractor’s risk assessment must
include the following areas:
(cid:131) Penetrations
(cid:131) Excavations
2.4.5 Fall Protection Plan and Working at Heights
The Principal Contractor/Contractors will in Writing appoint a Competent Fall Protection plan developer
with Knowledge, training and Experience (Valid Certificate) The fall protection Plan must be in line with
regulation 10 of the Construction Regulation of 2014. The plan must be revised on a monthly basis. The
Fall Protection Plan must include:
(cid:131) Risk Assessment for fall risk.
(cid:131) Training of persons working at height. (Fall Plan and Rescue Plan)
(cid:131) Procedure for inspecting, testing and maintenance of equipment.
(cid:131) The evaluation of employee’s medical fitness for working at height. (Include medication and
fasting).
(cid:131) Access to work area.
(cid:131) Rescue plan.
(cid:131) Method of safeguarding fall Risk.
(cid:131) Written Safe work Procedure
(cid:131) Proof of communication to employees
2.4.6 Scaffolding
All scaffolding must comply with the requirements of SANS 085. Principal Contractor must in writing
appoint a Competent Scaffolding inspector for this project. This appointment must form part of the
Safety File. The Principal Contractor must in writing appoint at least 1 competent scaffold erectors to
this Project. The appointment must form part of the file.
All scaffolding platforms above 2m from the ground must be complete with guardrails and toe boards
and must be fully boarded at the level from which the work will be undertaken as per the requirements
of general-purpose scaffold platforms. Mobile or Tower scaffolds may not exceed 3 X their minimum
Page 26 of 43

OCCUPATIONAL HEALTH AND SAFETY SPECIFICATION
25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),
STONEY POINT ROAD, BETTY'S BAY, OVERSTRAND MUNICIPALITY, WESTERN CAPE, SOUTH AFRICA
base width in height and must be adequately boarded as per their loading requirement (no less than 3-
board wide). Mobile scaffolds and static frame towers must be erected as per the manufacturer’s
requirements (copies of these erection specifications/data sheets must be available on site).
Any scaffold structures must be erected by a competent scaffold erector. Scaffolding must be declared
safe for use by a competent scaffold inspector who must complete the scaffold hand-over certificate.
Inspections must thereafter be carried out weekly, after bad weather, after any alterations, after an
incident, and before dismantling. The Principal Contractor must keep all scaffold inspection registers on
site.
All persons working in elevated positions must be assessed / evaluated for physical and psychological
fitness. Proof of these assessments / evaluations must be contained within the site safety file. All
persons working in elevated positions must be trained and records of this training must be kept on site.
Work from elevated positions may only be conducted from a safe ladder or safe scaffold. Where fall
prevention or fall arrest devices are being used, the correct devices must be used for the purpose and
they must be properly maintained. Workers must be trained in the use and Maintenance of the fall
prevention and arrest equipment/devices.
Where work is taking place in an elevated position, the area below must be adequately demarcated,
preventing persons from walking or working directly below. Where scaffolding is erected adjacent to or
over public areas and roadways, adequate public protection measures must be put in place. Pavement
gantries, fans/aprons, scaffold sheeting and/or closure/diversion of the public from the area are
considered the accepted measures.
No materials may be thrown down from platforms,, decks or any other elevated position unless a
demarcated area has been established on ground level to prevent other employees or persons from
walking out into the area to which the materials is being thrown.
2.4.7 Roof work
Not though to be applicable.
2.4.8 Temporary Work / Formwork and Support Work
The Principal Contractor/Contractor shall ensure that the provisions of section 12 of the Construction
Regulations (February 2014) are adhered to.
The Principal Contractor/ Contractor will appoint in writing a Temporary Works Designer. This
Appointment must form part of the Safety File. The Client Agent will not take responsibility for any
temporary works.

25139C – STONEY POINT BOARDWALK REPLACEMENT: STONEY POINT NATURE RESERVE (PENGUIN SANCTUARY),

The Principal Contractor/Contractor will be required to formulate a Safe Work Procedure for the
placement and dismantling of form work. This safe work procedure must be relevant to the form work
system used on the project. The Principal Contractor/Contractor must appoint a person with sufficient
knowledge and experience of this activity as the Form Work and Support Work Supervisor. Proof of
competency may be requested by the Safety Representative. A copy of this appointment must be placed
on the Safety File. The Construction Supervisor is to ensure that movement of mobile plant and mobile
pumps are controlled when pouring of concrete is in process. Proper communication between the Form
Work Supervisor and the pump operator is essential. The area underneath the cast must be demarcated
with no access during the pour. The form work and support work supervisor will be required to do pre
and post casting inspections with records kept on the Safety File. All form work must be in line with the
specification provided by the structural engineer. Support work must be secured, properly braced and
checked on a daily basis until back propping can be removed. Removal of propping may only be done on
instruction from the structural engineer and once the concrete has properly cured.
2.4.9 Stacking of Materials and Housekeeping
The Contractor will ensure that they appoint a Stacking and Storage Supervisor. This Appointment will
form part of the Safety file. The Supervisor will make sure all materials and all equipment is stacked and
stored safely, on level, compacted ground, out of access ways Walkways must be clear of all materials.
No materials may be stacked for storage on scaffold platforms, No materials may be stacked on the edge
of any deck, and penetration, balcony, loading platform or drop-off. A safe clearance of 1.5 meters from
any such edge must be maintained and where materials have been deposited near such an edge they
must immediately be removed. The Principal Contractor will be required to appoint a Housekeeping
Supervisor to ensure all rubble is removed from site and contractors comply with the environmental
regulations, if the Principal Contractors fails to maintain good housekeeping the Client Agent has the
right and responsibility to stop the contract until all waste and pathways are cleared from rubble.
2.4.10 Electrical Supply (Temporary), Distribution and Installations
All electrical work on this project must be done by a competent electrical company registered with the
Electrical Contracting Board of SA and appointed in writing as the Electrical Installations and Machinery
Inspector. This appointment must form part of the Safety File. All electrical work must comply with the
following:
(cid:131) All electrical installations must have an electrical compliance certificate. This will apply to the site
office as well.
(cid:131) All electrical installations (distribution boards) must be fitted with an earth
leakage system.

be properly fitted or fitted with a tripod.
