2.4.11 Hazardous Chemical Agents
The Principal Contractor and other relevant Contractors must provide the necessary training and
information as far as the use, transport, and storage of HCS. The Principal Contractor shall ensure that
the use, transport, and storage of HCS are carried out as prescribed in the HCS Regulations. The
Contractor shall ensure that all Hazardous Chemical Agents on site have Material Safety Data Sheets
(MSDS) on site and the users are made aware of the hazards and precautions that need to be taken
when using the chemicals.
The risk assessment must address the following items:
(cid:120) The HCS’s that employees may be exposed to;
(cid:120) The effects that the HCS can have on the employee;
(cid:120) Where the HCS may be present and in what physical form;
(cid:120) The route of intake;
(cid:120) The nature of the work and process involved.
The First Aiders must be made aware of the MSDS and how to treat HCS incidents accordingly.
Flammable substances must be stored in a separate area, away from other materials.
2.4.12 Batch Plant and Mixers
All Batch Plant must comply with the following: All batch plant operations must be done under the
supervision of a competent person appointed in writing. Placement of batch plant must be in
accordance with the manufacturer’s specification. Access to the plant must be kept clear at all times. All
batch plant need to be inspected on a daily basis and records of these inspections must form part of the
Safety File. All persons involved in the process need to wear the correct PPE.
2.4.13 Brick Work
All brickwork needs to be done from a safe platform such as scaffolding. The Construction Work
Manager is to ensure that the amount of bricks loaded on the scaffold is not excessive so as to overload
the structure. All bricks not used must be removed from the scaffold after use or at the end of the day.

Not thought to be applicable.
2.4.15 Demolition Work
The Principal Contractor must ensure that a demolition method statement be produced for the work to
be done. This document must highlight:
(cid:131) The sequence of demolition
(cid:131) The method of demolition
(cid:131) Propping of elements where and if required
(cid:131) Access of plant and vehicles to site/removal of rubble
(cid:131) Placement of plant
(cid:131) Hoarding layout where deviation of site hoarding is required
(cid:131) Protection of other structures
(cid:131) Dust Management
This method statement must be approved by the structural engineer as well as the client before work
may commence.
All demolitions must be done under the supervision of a competent person with the required knowledge
of the activity. This person must be appointed in writing as the Demolitions Supervisor and a copy of
this appointment must be placed in the Safety File.
All areas being demolished will be demarcated to restrict access. Only authorized persons may enter
these areas and only on approval of the Demolitions Supervisor. Demolished material will be removed as
a matter of urgency so as not to hinder the demolition process.
2.5 Plant and Machinery
2.5.1 Construction Plant & Small Plant
“Construction Plant” includes all types of plant including but not limited to, cranes, piling rigs,
excavators, road vehicles, dumpers and all lifting equipment.
The Principal Contractor /Contractors will be required to formulate a Written Safe Work Procedure to
ensure that all such plant complies with the requirements of the OHS Act 85/1993 and Construction

Regulations (February 2014). The Principal Contractor and all Contractors shall inspect and keep records
of inspections and load tests of the construction plant used on site. The inspection register must be
plant specific and the plant must be referenced on the register.
Only authorized/competent persons may use machinery and the proper supervision must be provided.
Appropriate PPE and clothing must be provided and maintained in good condition at all times.
No employees may travel as passengers on any mobile plant or construction vehicle unless they are
seated in the cab.
Small plant must be inspected before use and weekly thereafter for as long as it remains on site. The
inspection register must be plant specific.
All small plant must be in a safe for use condition. Small plant operators must also be provided with the
necessary PPE and specifically hearing protection.
2.5.2 Vessels under Pressure (VuP) and Gas Bottles
The Principal Contractor and all relevant Contractors shall comply with the Vessels under Pressure
Regulations, including:
(cid:131) Providing competency and awareness training to the operators;
(cid:131) Providing PPE
(cid:131) Inspect equipment regularly and keep records of inspections;
(cid:131) Providing appropriate firefighting equipment (Fire Extinguishers);
(cid:131) Oxygen and acetylene bottles must be secured in an upright position and must not show signs of
corrosion or damage.
2.5.3 Fire Extinguishers and Fire Fighting Equipment
The Principal Contractor /Contractors must in writing appoint a Fire Equipment Inspector. This
Appointment must form part of the Safety file The Principal Contractor shall provide adequate, regularly
serviced firefighting equipment located at strategic points on site, specific to the classes of fire likely to
occur. The appropriate notices and signs must be posted as required. A minimum of two 9kg dry
chemical powder units are required.
Wherever hot work is taking place, additional fire extinguishers must be on hand. Contractors are
responsible for ensuring compliance with hot work procedures and must be in possession of method
statements detailing the safe working procedures.

2.5.4 Hired Plant and Machinery
The Principal Contractor shall ensure that any hired plant and machinery used on site is safe for use and
complies with the minimum legislation requirements. The necessary requirements as stipulated by the
OHS Act 85/1993 and Construction Regulations (February 2014) shall apply. The Principal Contractor
shall ensure that operators hired with machinery are competent and that certificates are kept on site in
the health & safety file.
Any load test requirements and inspections in terms of legislation must be complied with and copies of
load test certificates and inspections must be kept in the health & safety file. All relevant Contractors
must ensure the same.
2.5.5 Ladders and Ladder Work
The Principal Contractor/Contractors must appoint a Ladder Inspector in writing, this appointment must
form part of the Safety File. The Principle Contractor / Contractors must ensure that all ladders are
inspected daily before use, are in good safe working order, are the correct height for the task, extend at
least 900 mm above the landing, fastened and secured, and at a safe angle. Stepladders must be safe for
use, must be the correct height for the task and the top two rungs may not be used. Records of
inspections must be kept in a register on site. Contractors using their own ladders must ensure the
same.

2.5.6.1 Mobile Crane
Not thought to be applicable for this project.

Not thought to be applicable for this project.

Not thought to be applicable for this project.
2.5.8 Loading Platforms
Should these platforms be used, they must carry a design drawing issued by a competent person
indicating the maximum safe workload and the erection and maintenance procedures. The platform
must be complete with guardrails and toe boards and must carry a notice indicating the maximum safe

2.6.0 Occupational Hygiene
The Principal Contractor and all Contractors are to ensure that proper health and hygiene measures are
put in place to prevent exposure to these hazards and risks. The occupational hazards and risks may
enter the body in three ways: Inhalation, Ingestion and Absorption. All Contractors should ensure that
Occupational Hygiene surveys are conducted as per the Occupational Health and Safety Act to ensure
employees are not exposed to hazards. Risk Assessments should identify areas where surveys are to be
conducted.
2.6.1 Occupational Hygiene
Exposure of workers to occupational health hazards and risks is very common in any work environment,
especially in construction. Occupational exposure is a major problem and all Contractors must ensure
that proper health and hygiene measures are put in place to prevent exposure to these hazards. Prevent
inhalation, ingestion, absorption, and noise induction.
2.6.2 Welfare Facilities
The Principal Contractor must supply sufficient toilets (1 toilet per 30 workers), changing facilities, hand
washing facilities, soap, toilet paper, and hand drying material must be provided. Waste bins must be
strategically placed and emptied regularly. Safe, clean storage areas must be provided for workers to
store personal belongings and personal protective equipment. Workers should not be exposed to
hazardous materials/substances while eating and must be provided with adequate, sheltered eating
areas.
2.6.3 Alcohol, Drugs and Smoking
No alcohol and other drugs will be allowed on site. No person may be under the influence of alcohol or
any other drugs while on the construction site. Any person on prescription drugs must inform his/her
superior, who shall in turn report this to the Principal Contractor forthwith. Any person suffering from
any illness/condition that may have a negative effect on his/her safety performance must report this to
his/her superior, who shall in turn report this to the Principal Contractor forthwith. Any person
suspected of being under the influence of alcohol or other drugs must be sent home immediately, to
report back the next day for a preliminary inquiry. The Contractor concerned must follow a full
disciplinary procedure and a copy of the disciplinary action must be forwarded to the Principal

Contractor for his records. No Smoking is permitted on site. There must be a designated area for
smoking. With signage.
2.7 Duties of the Designers
Construction Regulation 6 requires a Designer to do the following:
The designer of a structure must- (a) ensure that the applicable safety standards incorporated into these
Regulations under section 44 of the Act are complied with in the design; (b) take into consideration the
health and safety specification submitted by the client; (c) before the contract is put out to tender, make
available in a report to the client- (i) all relevant health and safety information about the design of the
relevant structure that may affect the pricing of the construction work; (ii) the geotechnical-science
aspects, where appropriate; and (iii) the loading that the structure is designed to withstand; (d) inform
the client in writing of any known or anticipated dangers or hazards relating to the construction work,
and make available all relevant information required for the safe execution of the work upon being
designed or when the design is subsequently altered; (e) refrain from including anything in the design of
the structure necessitating the use of dangerous procedures or materials hazardous to the health and
safety of persons, which can be avoided by modifying the design or by substituting materials; (g) when
mandated by the client to do so, carry out the necessary inspections at appropriate stages to verify that
the construction of the relevant structure is carried out in accordance with his design: Provided that if
the designer is not so mandated, the client’s appointed agent in this regard is responsible to carry out
such inspections; (h) when mandated as contemplated in paragraph (g), stop any contractor from
executing any construction work which is not in accordance with the relevant design’s health and safety
aspects: Provided that if the designer is not so mandated, the client’s appointed agent in that regard
must stop that contractor from executing that construction work; 0) when mandated as contemplated in
paragraph (g), in his or her final inspection of the completed structure in accordance with the National
Building Regulations, include the health and safety aspects of the structure as far as reasonably
practicable, declare the structure safe for use, and issue a completion certificate to the client and a copy
thereof to the contractor; and (0) during the design stage, take cognizance of ergonomic design
principles in order to minimize ergonomic related hazards in all phases of the life cycle of a structure. (2)
The designer of temporary works must ensure that- (a) all temporary works are adequately designed so
that it will be capable of supporting all anticipated vertical and lateral loads that may be applied; (b) the
designs of temporary works are done with close reference to the structural design drawings issued by
the contractor, and in the event of any uncertainty consult the contractor; (c) all drawings and
calculations pertaining to the design of temporary works are kept at the office of the temporary works
designer and are made available on request by an inspector; and (d) the loads caused by the temporary
works and any imposed loads are clearly indicated in the design.

[3]
1. Introduction and Site Location
Stony Point Colony is located in the town of Betty’s Bay in the Overstrand Municipality and
lies adjacent to the proclaimed Betty’s Bay Marine Protected Area. The first African
penguin (Spheniscus demersus) nest at Stony Point was found in 1982 and the numbers slowly increased
to 35 breeding pairs in 1986. The site was established as a reserve in 1982 by the then-Department of
Nature and Environmental Conservation. The colony lies on the site of the old Waaygat Whaling
Station, which was used to harvest and process whale meat in the early to mid-1900s. Remnants of
the infrastructure for this industry which closed in 1930 can still be found at the site.
CapeNature took over the management of the Stony Point penguin colony at Betty’s Bay in 2016, it
was previously managed by the Overstrand Municipality. (cid:3031)CapeNature colonies currently support
approximately 50% of the remaining African Penguin population at three different colonies, one of
which is the Stony Point colony.
Figure 1. Map showing the location of Stony Point Nature Reserve

[4]
1. Project Description
The removal of the existing timber walkway and construction of a new structural steel and timber walkway,
consisting of concrete support columns, steel beam structure, steel balustrades. In addition to all other
contractual requirements, it is highlighted that the works are to be executed in accordance to Proposed New
Boardwalk Methodology Statement and the Environmental Management Plan (part of this tender document)
Figure 2: Diagram of approved boardwalk
Purpose of the Environmental Management Program
The main purpose of an Environmental Management Programme (EMP) is to identify key environmental
risks & hazards and prevent avoidable damage and/or minimise or mitigate unavoidable environmental
damage associated with a construction, maintenance, or demolition project/works.
An EMP is required as part of the application process for any project triggering environmental
authorisation, but must also be used for any project with risk of environmental harm in our protected
areas.
The EMP forms part of the contractual obligations to which all contractors/employees involved in
construction, maintenance, or demolition work must be committed.
The EMP facilitates and reminds contractors to comply with all applicable laws, regulations, standards
and guidelines for the protection of the environment; and adopts best practise principles for
construction, upgrades and maintenance in Protected Areas.
This EMP:
(cid:120) identifies important and sensitive species and environments near project site/s that could
feasibly be affected by works;

[5]
(cid:120) identifies project activities that could cause environmental damage (risks) and provides a
summary of actions required;
(cid:120) identifies persons responsible for ensuring compliance with the EMP and provides their
contact information;
(cid:120) provides standard procedures to avoid and/or minimise the identified negative environmental
impacts and to enhance the positive impact of the project on the environment;
(cid:120) provides site and project specific rules and actions required, including a site plan/s showing:
o areas where construction, maintenance, or demolition work may be carried out;
o areas where any material or waste may be stored;
o allowed access routes, parking and turning areas for construction or construction
related vehicles;
(cid:120) forms a written record of procedures, responsibilities, requirements and rules for staff, and
any other person who must comply with the EMP;
(cid:120) provides a monitoring and auditing programme to track and record compliance and identify
and respond to any potential or actual negative environmental impacts; and
(cid:120) provides a monitoring programme to record any mitigation measures that are implemented.
1. Roles and Responsibilities
As the client, CapeNature must take final responsibility for compliance monitoring of
this EMP and its requirements including any environmental rehabilitation that may be needed.This
is mandated by Section 28 (Duty of Care and Remediation of Damage) of the National Environmental
Management Act, (Act No. 107 of 1998).

Environmental Control Officer (ECO):Independent person to be appointed
The ECO must be independent and have expertise in conducting environmental compliance auditing,
including knowledge of the EIA Regulations and any other environmental legislation, guidelines and
policies related to environmental management3.
The ECO must be proactive and have access to specialist expertise as and when required. The ECO
must conduct audits on compliance to the CEMP for the project. Site inspections must be undertaken
weekly for the duration of construction.

[7]
The ECO must communicate and inform the Employer and Engineer of any changes to Environmental
Conditions as required by relevant authoritative bodies. The ECO must ensure that the updating of
all relevant CEMP documentation is carried out.
The ECO shall be responsible for monitoring, reviewing and verifying compliance with the CEMP by
the Contractor. The ECO's duties in this regard will include the following:
(cid:127) (cid:127) Monitoring and verifying that the CEMP is adhered to at all times and recommending
necessary action if the specifications are not followed;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Monitoring and verifying that environmental impacts are kept to a minimum;
(cid:127) (cid:127) The ECO along with the Engineer, must obtain, examine and approve Method Statements;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Assist the Contractor in finding environmentally responsible solutions to problems;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Report back on the environmental issues at the site meetings and other meetings that may
be called regarding environmental matters, if required;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Monitor and review the Site Diary of all activities / incidents / complaints concerning the
environment on Site;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Inspecting the Site and surrounding working areas with regard to compliance with the CEMP;
(cid:127) (cid:127) To be reachable by the public regarding matters of environmental concerns as they relate to
the development, however all communication to be undertaken in line with the approved CapeNature
communication protocols via the Corporate Communications Team.
(cid:127) (cid:127) Provide environmental awareness training for site personnel;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Recommend corrective action to the Engineer and the Contractor where construction
activities are not in compliance with the CEMP;
(cid:127) (cid:127) To inform the Engineer immediately of the occurrence of non-compliances and to
recommend appropriate measures of rectification, e.g. issuing of fines.
(cid:127) (cid:127) Ensure that activities on site comply with legislation of relevance to the environment;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Keep a photographic record of progress on Site from an environmental perspective;
(cid:127) (cid:127) Undertake weekly site visits and associated checklists; and
(cid:127) (cid:127) Compile and submit all monthly audit reports to the Employer, Engineer and Contractor.
(cid:127) (cid:127) Engage and liaise with CapeNature site patrolling the site on a daily basis for additional
feedback and input.

1. Potential Environmental Impacts, Risks and Threats
The potential environmental impacts as well as threats or risks are listed below. They refer to the
broad categories, which will be monitored throughout the project. It is important to note that impacts
rated as extreme, high, medium or low refer both to the severity of the negative impact as well as the
potential persistence of the impact.
Very High Impact: Human and/or animal life and property in danger, permanent behavioural
changes are seen in species breeding on site, significant cost implications. Impact may last for more
than 1 year
High Impact: Serious negative impact (life or property), serious behavioural changes seen in
species breeding on site, will need rehabilitation or remedial action (with cost implications). Impact
may last up to a year.
Medium Impact: Negative impact, which may have cost implications, temporary changes in
behaviour of species breeding on site, potential tourism impacts. Impacts may last up to three (3)
months.
Low Impact: Some negative impacts, not likely to have cost implications. Impacts will be resolved
in less than 1 day.

1. Heritage Management
The Heritage Resources Act and permit conditions set by the managing authorities protects heritage
resources. Failure to plan for the protection of these areas will have legal implications

[10]
All staff and suppliers must ensure to remain in the approved areas and to be mindful to not cause
damage to any objects or structures that have historic significance.
1. OHS Management
Every contractor and sub-contractor is required by the Occupational Health and Safety Act 85 of 1993
and the Construction Regulations 2014, to have a Safety File that must be available at all times. The
Occupational Health and Safety Act 85 of 1993 and Construction Regulations 2014 state the following:
Construction Regulation 3(6)– A client must ensure that the principle contractor keeps a copy of the
construction work permit contemplated in sub-regulation (1) in the occupational health and safety file
for inspection by an inspector, the client, the client’s authorized agent, or an employee; Construction
Regulation 5(1)(s) – The client must ensure that health and safety file contemplated in regulation
7(1)(b) is kept and maintained by the principal contractor; Construction Regulation 7(1)(b) –A
principal contractor must open and keep on-site a health and safety file which must include all
documentation required in terms of the Act and these Regulations, which must be made available on
request to an inspector, the client, the client’s agent or a contractor. The Health and Safety File must
be maintained by the site safety personnel and audited by a competent person.
The OHS Officer is responsible to ensure that the OHS Files and Systems are put in place and available
on site. The office must also ensure that all methods statements and works on site are in line with safe
work procedures. PPE must be supplied and correctly worn or used to mitigate incidents and
accidents.
The OHS file must include at minimum the following:
(cid:120) Copy of the OHS Act
(cid:120) Occupational Health and Safety Management Plan
(cid:120) Company Occupational Health and Safety Policy
(cid:120) Letter of Good Standing
(cid:120) Material Safety Data Sheets for hazardous materials used (if required)
(cid:120) Risk Assessments
(cid:120) Safe work procedures (Site Specific)
(cid:120) Fall Protection plan (if required)
(cid:120) Legal appointment with proof of training (Ex. Chief Executive Officer, Risk Assessor, First
Aider, etc.)
(cid:120) Incident reporting procedures
(cid:120) Incident reports (General Administrative Regulation 9 (3) – Annexure 1)
(cid:120) Incident registers
(cid:120) Reports of accidents
(cid:120) Emergency preparedness documents
(cid:120) First aid documents
(cid:120) Induction records
(cid:120) Medical surveillance records
(cid:120) Safety communication (e.g. Toolbox talks)
(cid:120) Minutes of safety meetings (toolbox talks)

[11]
(cid:120) Inspection registers
(cid:120) Copies of vehicle and trailer roadworthy certificates and licenses
NB: The team representative do attend the CapeNature OHS monthly meeting and report back to
the meeting on any OHS incident and updates from the construction site.

(cid:120) Work stoppages
(cid:120) Prosecution in terms of Environmental Legislation (Fines/Arrests)
(cid:120) Rehabilitation work, invoiced to the Contractor

1. Induction
Induction and EMP training will be done by the ECO prior to work commencement. All staff who will
access or work on the site will be required to attend, including any project supervising staff and OHS
appointed staff. Access to the site will not be granted to staff who have not attended the training.
The purpose of the Induction and training is to create awareness about the Hottentots Holland Nature
Reserve, the sensitivity of the site, the risks and hazards, the content, terms and conditions of the EMP
and Site rules.

[13]
1. EMP Checklist and Environmental Register
This checklist prompts the ECO in terms of what to check during site inspections and will be the
Report Format used. The EMP checklist must be completed each time the ECO comes to do site
inspections, copies of the report will be submitted to the Principle Agent and the Environmental
Specialist.
An environmental register must be provided by the Principal Agent and always kept on-site as well as
being freely accessible to all project team members.
In the event of any environmental incidents, the Environmental Register must be completed by the
most senior person on site: the Principal Agent, the Engineer or the Site Manager. The Environmental
Register may also be completed by the ECO if the ECO is on site when the incident occurs.
The register will provide a record of all environmental incidents that occur as a result of the on-site
activity. This may include information related to such aspects as spillages, dust generation and
complaints from adjacent neighbours and any other environmental incidents. It must also contain
information relating to action taken/mitigation measures employed.
10.EMP terms and conditions
10.1. Access - General
Access must be carefully managed to mitigate against:
(cid:120) Unacceptable noise, vibration and visual disturbance to African penguins and other fauna in
the area
(cid:120) The uncontrolled pedestrian or vehicular access to construction area
(cid:120) Unmanaged parking & traffic build up
(cid:120) Blocking access (impacting deliveries, normal work functions or rapid evacuation in case of
emergency)
(cid:120) Damage to vegetation
(cid:120) Accidents
(cid:120) Loss of soil
(cid:120) Negative visitor experiences
Access to the site is subject to the following conditions:
(cid:120) Access to the construction site is only for authorized personnel from CapeNature and the
appointed contractor (staff and suppliers).
(cid:120) Access attendance registers must be kept on site.
(cid:120) Access is permitted Monday to Fridays,10:00 – 14:30 (workers must stop construction activities
by 14:00 and complete clear up by 14:30)

[14]
(cid:120) Activities which create loud noises and/or vibration are not permitted during the first breeding
attempt period or moulting season. This is to mitigate against noise pollution and disturbance
which penguins are highly sensitive to. This means that no drilling or other noisy activities may
take place from November to February. Other construction activities may be permitted which
do not create noise or vibration but must be closely monitored.
(cid:120) Working on weekends is permitted, within the same limited hours, subject to consultation with
the Conservation Manager when this is needed and ensuring that such work does not include
operating noise generating machinery and tools.
(cid:120) All contractors, subcontractors and staff must be easily identified by clothing with company logos
and be in possession of valid SA identity documents.
(cid:120) Deliveries of new construction materials, removal of old material etc. to be completed during
between 10:00 – 14:30 Monday to Friday.
(cid:120) All vehicles and access to the site must remain within demarcated access routes and working
areas on site.
(cid:120) No new roads or tracks may be created
(cid:120) Vehicle access is restricted to the existing parking areas.
(cid:120) Offroad driving or parking is not permitted
(cid:120) Damage shall not be permitted to the road or tracks and any damages caused shall be repaired
by the contractor to the specifications set by the Principal Agent.
(cid:120) All vehicles must be in a roadworthy conditions and drivers must have the required licenses.
10.2. Demarcated Areas, Parking and Turning Areas
Construction and storage areas, as identified by the Conservation Manager (ECO) must be physically
demarcated on site (by the contractor). The Senior Site Supervisor and Project Manager will ensure
that the construction team, machinery and equipment stay within the working areas and do not
encroach on “no-go” areas.
We suggest phasing of the construction due to the restricted time periods that work can occur in.
Construction should begin at Point A (refer to Figure 3 below) and the first phase should entail
completion of the section from Point A to Point B. This may mean that construction of the entire
boardwalk may need to be completed over 2 or more years but it is imperative that no disturbance
occurs from December to March, especially between Points B and C. Each section must be further
sub-divided into small screened areas and the screens must be moved concurrently with construction
activities.

[15]
Figure 3: Aerial imagery (GoogleEarthPro) showing the track of the existing boardwalk at Stony
Point.
In addition:
(cid:120) Movement must be to restricted to the boardwalk for access to the worksite which may only
be a small section of the new boardwalk at a time that is screened (as per above) where
construction is taking place. The screen needs to be flush with the ground to prevent penguins
accessing the work site. Refer to Figure 4 below for an example of a building site screen.
(cid:120) Hazard tape is not permitted as the site is windy and broken tape poses a hazard to birds.
(cid:120) Attaching or fixing of any fencing, signs or other materials on natural item (Vegetation, Rocks
etc) is not permitted other than what is shown in the approved structure diagram (as shown
in Figure 2 on page 4).
(cid:120) Contractors may not store any construction material in any other area which is not within
the screened area where it could impede normal access or cause any negative impact to nature
or other user groups.
Figure 4: Example of a building site screen

[16]
An independent ECO with appropriate experience working in this type of environment and with
penguins must visit the site at least weekly while construction is taking place. In addition, CapeNature
must maintain sufficient capacity of staff with the appropriate ecological knowledge and experience
working with penguins to patrol the site daily.
10.3 Fauna and Flora
Construction Activities, if unmanaged will cause negative impacts to plants and animals (as well as the
natural environment in general). Such damages could impact specific species (plant or animal)
ecosystem functioning, lead to biodiversity loss. There is also the extended implications in terms of
reputational harm to CapeNature and the Contractor.
The objective with mitigating measures is to prevent any damage or harm to the natural environment,
especially fauna and flora.
Mitigation measures include:
(cid:120) Ensure construction footprint does not include any nesting sites
(cid:120) Avoid permanently blocking access paths used by penguins to move to and from the ocean
(cid:120) Demarcate no-go areas
(cid:120) No item, including signs, fencing or posts may be erected against any natural vegetation
(cid:120) No vehicles are permitted past the existing parking areas. Exception may be made if necessary
but no further than concrete area in front of the restaurant and only after consultation with
the reserve manager of conservation officer.
(cid:120) Trampling, cutting or other damage of any natural vegetation is not permitted
(cid:120) Animals (all species including mammals, birds, insects, snakes, spiders etc) are protected and
may not be harmed or disturbed in any way.
(cid:120) Animals may not be lured or fed
(cid:120) Should any animal enter the construction area and become a hazard to the work continuing,
work should cease and the ECO must be contacted immediately to ensure that the animal is
relocated or deterred by an appropriate professional.
Rehabilitation and Penalties: Any negligent and wilful damage or harm shall require the contractor/
official to pay for rehabilitation costs. Prosecution may also follow, depending on nature of damage.
10.4 Noise and Disturbance
In addition to the site being in Stony Point Nature Reserve, there is a restaurant, parking areas, offices
and meeting venues in close proximity to the construction site.
Mitigation Measures:
(cid:120) Music is not permitted
(cid:120) Shouting (except to prevent a serious accident) is not permitted
(cid:120) Hooting and revving of vehicles are not permitted.
(cid:120) Communication noise levels should be appropriate for work required.

[17]
(cid:120) Generators may not be used unless they are silent generators or inverters with zero sound.
(cid:120) Disruptive behaviour, arguments, verbal or physical conflict must be avoided completely.
10.5 Litter & Waste Management
Construction activities, by their very nature generate litter or waste. This must be carefully managed
to ensure that at no point there is waste or litter impact on the natural environment.
Mitigation Measures:
(cid:120) No waste materials or liquids, including contaminated waste water may be disposed of on site,
neither in the designated working area/s, nor any no-go areas.
(cid:120) Waste must be held onsite in sealed storage containers and properly disposed of at a
designated waste facility.
(cid:120) No exceptions will be made.
(cid:120) Enough waste bins / bags must be provided by the contractor
(cid:120) This litter must be disposed of to the approved local municipal site.
(cid:120) Special attention must be paid to cement bags, plastics, materials packaging etc.
(cid:120) Special care must be taken to ensure construction materials (broken planks, nails, screws,
cabling, wires, plumbing accessories etc) are left on site. A thorough clean up must be done
daily.
(cid:120) Hazardous waste (chemicals, medical etc) must be taken to a suitable waste disposal site that
is registered to deal with it. This is the responsibility of the Contractor.
(cid:120) Bins must be cleaned and serviced regularly (absolute minimum of weekly) to ensure there is
not build up of waste and it does not attract pests (insects, worms, flies etc).
(cid:120) Waste must be contained at all times. There should be no loose bags or packet lying around.
(cid:120) Bins need to have lids and waste storage / sorting areas enclosed to prevent wind-blown litter
and to prevent animals from accessing it.
(cid:120) Waste bins to be secured where necessary
(cid:120) No waste shall be illegally dumped, burnt or buried, but disposed to an adequate waste facility.
(cid:120) Records of waste disposal shall be kept for audit purposes.
(cid:120) Recycling: Where possible recycle wastewater, plastics, paper, wood, scrap metal,
compostable waste etc.
(cid:120) There are sufficient toilets on site and using the natural environment as a toilet is not only
illegal, but also unacceptable.
10.6 Fires
Wildfires can cause major damage and loss to human life, vegetation loss, wildlife loss and tourism
infrastructure. These losses are very costly in terms of suppression, rehabilitation and reconstruction
activities.
Any veldfire which is the result of potential negligence or failure to act in terms of this EMP may result
in costs being recovered from the Contractor as well as further prosecution.

[18]
Mitigation Measures include:
(cid:120) Smoking is only permitted in designated locations.
(cid:120) No cooking or other fires are permitted.
(cid:120) Smoking is not permitted outside the designated area or in any CapeNature facility
(cid:120) Heavy vehicles must have fire extinguishers.
(cid:120) All precautions must be taken to avoid accidental fires
(cid:120) Hotworks (e.g welding) must be pre-approved by the ECO and the method statement and
mitigation must be pre-approved.
11.Pre-construction phase
(cid:120) EMP checked and signed off
(cid:120) Specs supplied
(cid:120) OHS file updated and signed off
(cid:120) Induction completed
(cid:120) High risk activities listed and method statements provided
(cid:120) Confirm construction area on site
(cid:120) Confirm storage areas on site
(cid:120) Confirm No-Go areas on site
12.CONSTRUCTION PHASE – GENERAL TERMS AND CONDITIONS
13.1 CEMENT MIXING AND USE
The team is advised that cement and concrete are highly hazardous to the natural environment
because of the high pH levels of the material, and the chemicals contained therein.
The following shall apply:
(cid:120) Prevent cement pollution - ensure that soil and water is not contaminated with cement.
(cid:120) Premixed cement/concrete should be used on site
(cid:120) Concrete / cement may not be mixed on the site
(cid:120) Concrete spills (no matter how small) must be physically removed immediately and
disposed of as hazardous waste.
(cid:120) Washing of equipment must be done off site.
(cid:120) No equipment shall be washed in the sea or elsewhere within the natural environment

[19]
13.1. Fuel Management
Vehicles and machinery must be refulled and serviced outside of the boundaries of Stony Point Nature
Reserve.
13.2. Chemicals and other hazardous materials
Should the contractor be required to make use of any chemical, corrosive substance or potentially
hazardous product, this must be declared to the OHS officer and ECO, before the items is brought on
site. Methods statements on the handle, use and storage of the items must be approved by the OHS
officer. There will need to be toolbox talks regarding these items use and PPE should be in place and
correctly used.
*Failure to comply with special activity method statements and EMP conditions will result in
immediate work stoppage, require special cleaning operations and may result in further prosecution
in severe cases.
14.Post construction phase
(cid:120) Pre-final site inspection and snagging – Tourism Officer; ECO & Project Team
(cid:120) Final site inspection – Tourism Officer; ECO & Project Team
(cid:120) Safe decommissioning of site (Removal of all construction items, materials, vehicles; full site
clean up etc)
(cid:120) Site sign off – Principle agent & ECO
(cid:120) Site handover from Tourism Development to Conservation Operations in safe and fully
operational state.
15.Contract Obligations
It is understood that all contract documentation related to the construction, operation and
decommissioning (if required) of the proposed development will include the conditions of this EMP.
It is important to note that the contract obligations must include the recording of any complaints on
the project in the environmental register (defined below). It is the responsibility of the ECO to keep
an accurate audit trail showing compliance with the EMP during construction phase.

[20]
ANNEXURE 1 – SITE RULES
A copy of these rules must be posted at the work site and be complied with by all staff entering the work area.
1. Comply with all environmental legislation (NEMA, NEM:PAA; NEM:BA; Conservation Ordinance as it
applies in terms of site access and environmental protected
1. Comply with all mitigation measures as listed in this EMP
2. Remain within designated construction site and work areas
3. Storage only permitted in designated work areas
4. All natural areas outside the construction area are considered “no-go” areas
5. Damage to or trampling of vegetation is not permitted
6. Luring, harming or killing of any wildlife (all vertebrates, invertebrates, including snakes, birds, buck etc)
is not permitted
1. Fishing or removing any type of aquatic life is not permitted
2. Swimming in the river is not permitted (this is to avoid water contamination)
3. Water sources may not be contaminated in any way or form
4. Use water responsibly and sparingly
5. Washing off of paintbrushes, cement and other contaminants may not take place in the natural
environment or anywhere where it could run off to the veld or water. NB contaminated water must be
put into containers and disposed at a approved waste removal site
1. Abstraction of water from natural water sources (excluding the provided taps) are not permitted
2. Damage to geological or cultural historic structures are not permitted
3. No natural items (resources, including wood, plants, animals or parts thereof) may be removed
4. Smoking is only allowed in the designated smoking area.
5. Litter may not under any circumstances be allowed to blow around the site
6. Littering is not permitted
7. Use of any place for ablutions, other than the approved ablutions are not allowed
8. Generators and other tools must be used in accordance with the EMP and safety specifications
9. Spillages of any kind is prohibited (Waste water, cement, fuel, chemicals)
10. Noise pollution is not permitted.
11. Light pollution is not permitted – use minimum lights to avoid disturbance to other users, wildlife or
the dark skies principle.
1. Parking is only allowed in approved bays.
2. Cement, stone and sand must be covered to avoid being rained on, washed away or blown away
3. Demolitions / explosives of ANY kind is strictly prohibited
4. Be respectful to the staff, residents, guests and other users at all times.
5. Follow the instructions of the OHS officer on site
6. Follow the instructions of the ECO on site, especially where it pertains to environmental safety & access
7. Be aware of bees and snakes in the area, do not antagonize or in any way harm them. Call the ECO if
you have a problem
1. Domestic animals and plants may not be brought into Stony Point Nature Reserve
2. Only appointed staff and suppliers may enter the area, not guests or visitors. This includes family or
friends of the staff, the purpose is access for work purposes.
1. Vehicles must be fully roadworthy, leak free and operated responsibly. No hooting, revving or driving
around after the designated work hours, except in case of necessity. NB This speaks to reducing
disturbance to other uses and neighbours through noise or light pollution

[21]
ANNEXURE 2: NATIONAL ENVIRONMENTAL MANAGEMENT ACT PRINCIPLES
The NEMA Principles states that sustainable development requires the consideration of all relevant
factors including the following:
(cid:120) Disturbance of ecosystems and loss of biological diversity must be prevented, or, where they
cannot be altogether avoided, must be minimised and remedied;
(cid:120) Pollution and degradation of the environment must be avoided, or, where they cannot be
altogether avoided, must be minimised and remedied;
(cid:120) Disturbance of landscapes and sites that constitute the nation's cultural heritage must be
avoided, or where it cannot be altogether avoided, must be minimised and remedied;
(cid:120) Waste must be avoided, or where it cannot be altogether avoided, minimised and re-used or
recycled where possible and otherwise dispose of in a responsible manner;
(cid:120) Use and exploitation of non-renewable natural resources must be responsible and equitable,
and take into account the consequences of the depletion of the resource;
(cid:120) Development, use and exploitation of renewable resources and the ecosystems of which they
are part must not exceed the level beyond which their integrity is jeopardised;
(cid:120) A risk-averse and cautious approach must be applied, that takes into account the limits of
current knowledge about the consequences of decisions and actions; and
(cid:120) Negative impacts on the environment and on people's environmental rights must be
anticipated and prevented, and where they cannot be altogether prevented, must be
minimised and remedied.
